**R31** — Part X, Newness. Declaration: "Part X now defines the equivalence in (N): d ≡_ℓ c when there are transports from d to c and from c to d, both faithful on c's contract at grain ℓ. The equivalence is structural at that grain, not string equality or similarity."

New wording: "**Newness.** Write \(d\equiv_\ell c\) when there are transports from \(d\) to \(c\) and from \(c\) to \(d\), both faithful on \(c\)'s contract at grain \(\ell\). The equivalence is structural at the stated grain, not string equality or similarity. With \(R_{<e}(s,h)=\bigcup_{\xi\text{ before }e}R_{\beta,\ell}(s,\xi)\),"

(a) Declaration matches. But — is the defined relation actually an equivalence? "Write d≡_ℓ c when there are transports from d to c and from c to d, both faithful on c's contract at grain ℓ." Reflexivity: d ≡ d requires transports from d to d both ways, faithful on d's contract. Is the identity transport faithful? Presumably yes (assuming the identity satisfies F1, F2, A — yes, with π=id, τ=id, σ=id, λ = each component anchored to itself). Hmm, but F1 requires "for every active component k of E with anchor subnetwork λ(k) ⊆ D": proj_{V_k}[Sol_{λ(k)}(a,b)] = L_k(τ(a), σ(b)). With identity transport and λ(k) = {k}, Sol_{k}(a,b) restricted... hmm, Sol_{λ(k)}(a,b) is the solutions of the subnetwork; proj onto V_k gives... hmm, if λ(k) is just component k, then Sol_{λ(k)}(a,b) = L_k(a,b) projected? Actually Sol of the subnetwork = L_k(a,b) as valuations on V_k. proj onto V_k = L_k(a,b) = L_k(a,b). OK. And F2: π[Sol_D(a,b)] = Sol_E(...) — identity. Fine. So identity is faithful on any contract. But wait — faithfulness is "on C" where C is "c's contract" — for reflexivity d ≡ d we need transports faithful on d's contract. Fine.

Transitivity: d ≡ c (transports d→c and c→d faithful on c's contract) and c ≡ e (transports c→e and e→c faithful on e's contract). Then d ≡ e requires transports d→e and e→d faithful on e's contract. Composing d→c with c→e: is the composition faithful on e's contract? The transport d→c is faithful on c's contract, not necessarily on e's contract. So transitivity fails in general! The relation "both faithful on c's contract" is asymmetric in the reference to c's contract. Hmm, but the claim just says "Write d≡_ℓ c when..." — it names it "the equivalence" ("The equivalence is structural at the stated grain"). The declaration says "defines the equivalence in (N)". If the relation isn't an equivalence relation, that's a coherence defect. Is it? Let's double check symmetry: d ≡ c iff there are transports d→c and c→d both faithful on c's contract. c ≡ d iff there are transports c→d and d→c both faithful on d's contract. These can differ: d→c and c→d faithful on c's contract, but d→c not faithful on d's contract. So the relation is not symmetric either. Hmm.

Also "faithful on c's contract" — but a transport is faithful on C (a contract); here "faithful on c's contract at grain ℓ" — faithfulness was defined for transports on a contract C; (R) uses "Faithful_C(t: Org_ℓ(o) → c)". OK.

Calling it "the equivalence" asserts it's an equivalence relation (or at least treats it as one). (N) uses it: New(s,c,h,e) iff ¬∃d ∈ R_{<e}, d ≡_ℓ c. R22's addition to Part VI, Redundant routes: "Here a route is a support of the candidate, a member of \(\mathsf S\), and is a route whether or not any history runs it; it is active in a system when occurrences that realize it form an active route (Part IX), a subnetwork of actual occurrences in the system's history. The two meet in \(\operatorname{ProducedBy}\) (Part XI), which credits a repair to the contributions whose active routes ran to it, not to the routes a candidate contains."

Hmm! Now here's a potential clash. Part IX defines "active route" as "a connected subnetwork of actual occurrences joining a represented input to an operative result, whose components satisfy the applicable relations and which has nonconstant dependence on the represented distinction under the declared contrasts." And: "A route that started and did no work, or that was already at rest when the result occurred, is not active for that result".

R22 says "it is active in a system when occurrences that realize it form an active route (Part IX)". So "active" for a candidate route (member of S) is defined via Part IX's "active route". But note Part IX's term is "active route" and R22 uses "active route" to define when a "route" (a member of S) is "active". So the word "route" is used in two senses: (i) a support W ∈ S of the candidate (Part VI), (ii) a connected subnetwork of actual occurrences (Part IX). R22 explicitly disambiguates: "Here a route is a support of the candidate... it is active in a system when occurrences that realize it form an active route (Part IX)". OK.

But now — Part VI's "Redundant routes" paragraph originally (in the current text) said: "Two systems with the same output table may differ in which routes are active; the semantics represents the difference (Derivation 9)." So "routes" there meant... something. R22 clarifies.

Now, does R22's clarification change a claim? The declaration says: "Part VI now says four things. A route is a support of the candidate, a member of S, and is a route whether or not any history runs it. Actually hold on. Let me reconsider. Maybe the more serious issue with R31: the declaration says "defines the equivalence in (N)" and the new wording calls it "The equivalence". But the defined relation is NOT an equivalence relation (not symmetric — as I showed). Let me double check symmetry once more.

Definition: d ≡_ℓ c iff (i) there is a transport from d to c faithful on c's contract at grain ℓ; and (ii) there is a transport from c to d faithful on c's contract at grain ℓ.

Symmetry would require: d ≡_ℓ c iff c ≡_ℓ d. c ≡_ℓ d iff transports c→d and d→c both faithful on d's contract. Condition (i) of d≡c is a transport d→c faithful on c's contract; the corresponding condition of c≡d is a transport d→c faithful on d's contract. These differ. So symmetry fails whenever a transport is faithful on one contract but not the other. Since faithfulness is contract-relative and two contents can have different contracts, yes, symmetry can fail.

Concrete case: d and c are organizations with faithful transports both ways, where c's contract is coarser than d's contract. Suppose the transport d→c is faithful on c's coarse contract but not on d's finer contract; and the transport c→d is faithful on c's contract... hmm, a transport from c to d faithful on c's contract (source c, contract c's — properly typed). So d ≡ c holds (both faithful on c's contract: the d→c one on c's contract in the (R) sense, the c→d one properly). But c ≡ d requires both faithful on d's contract: the c→d transport must be faithful on d's contract — but d's contract is over d's edits and the source of c→d is c... same typing issue; and substantively, the c→d transport might not be faithful on d's finer contract (c's coarser structure can't reproduce d's finer responses). So c ≡ d fails while d ≡ c holds. Not symmetric.

"**Ownership.** The subhistory in Build is owned by \(s\) when its processes run inside the system boundary and resource contract declared for \(s\) (Part XII). **Candidate 1 (R31)**: The relation defined is not an equivalence (fails symmetry — "faithful on c's contract" anchors both directions to c's contract, so c ≡ d and d ≡ c come apart whenever the two contracts differ). The text calls it "The equivalence" and writes ≡. This is a (c) coherence defect (an assertion in the new sentence that is false of the defined relation; and it bears on (N), which needs a genuine equivalence for "new" to be well-behaved). Hmm — also, the definition is stated only for contents with contracts, while (G)/(N) allow c to be a transport or a contract (question-finding); "c's contract" is undefined for a contract-content. Hmm — that's a second (c) point (Derivation 5's question-finding case).

Hmm — is the symmetry issue real? Let me re-verify: "Write \(d\equiv_\ell c\) when there are transports from \(d\) to \(c\) and from \(c\) to \(d\), both faithful on \(c\)'s contract at grain \(\ell\)." — so the relation R(d,c) :=∃t₁: d→c faithful on C_c ∧ ∃t₂: c→d faithful on C_c. Symmetry requires R(d,c) = R(c,d), where R(c,d) := ∃t₁: c→d faithful on C_d ∧ ∃t₂: d→c faithful on C_d. These differ (C_c vs C_d). So R is not symmetric unless C_c = C_d or faithfulness on one entails faithfulness on the other. Concretely: if C_c is coarser than C_d, then a transport faithful on C_c might fail on C_d. So R(d,c) can hold while R(c,d) fails. Real. ✓.

Hmm — and is this a defect serious enough for R31 to FALL? Hmm — the declaration repeats "the equivalence" — hmm — so the declaration itself misdescribes?? Hmm — the declaration says "Part X now defines the equivalence in (N): d ≡_ℓ c when...". Hmm — if the relation isn't an equivalence, then the declaration's phrase "the equivalence" is just naming it. Hmm — the declaration faithfully describes the new wording (which calls it "The equivalence"). Hmm — so (a) is satisfied but (c) shows the text is wrong to call it an equivalence. Hmm — is that enough to FALL? Hmm — "it makes the text incoherent" — hmm — a mislabeled relation: (N) uses it as "d is equivalent to c" — if it's not symmetric, then "New" is asymmetric in a way that might matter... hmm — for (N)'s use (always with c in the second slot) it's fine. Hmm.

Hmm — hmm. Hmm — let me think about whether the asymmetry creates a real problem for (N). (N): New(s,c,h,e) iff no d in the prior repertoire with d ≡_ℓ c. Suppose the prior repertoire contains c's exact twin d (same content, different notation) but with a DIFFERENT contract (d's contract is finer). Hmm — then d ≡_ℓ c might hold (both faithful on c's coarse contract) → not new. Fine. Suppose the prior repertoire contains d with a COARSER contract: d ≡_ℓ c requires faithfulness on c's (finer) contract — the coarser d might fail → New holds. Hmm — is that right? If the prior content d is a coarser version of c (can't respond to c's finer changes), then c IS new (it does something d can't). Fine!! Hmm — so the asymmetric anchoring (to c's contract) is actually DOING THE RIGHT WORK for (N): "c is not new iff something already deployable does everything c does at c's contract". Hmm!!! So the relation is fine for (N) — it's just not an "equivalence". Hmm — so the defect is terminological ("The equivalence")? Hmm.

Read as the gloss it presents itself as, it lets any subnetwork of occurrences count as active, and then ProducedBy (via R22's last sentence) would credit both routes in O25 and O52 — moving the verdicts away from "Ana stopped it" and "Ben stopped it". Repair: delete the apposition or restore Part IX's conditions ("...form, for the result in question, an active route in the sense of Part IX — a connected subnetwork of actual occurrences joining a represented input to that operative result...").

2. **R31 (c+a)**: "The equivalence" is not an equivalence as defined (asymmetric anchoring to c's contract); and the definition covers only contents with a contract, while (N) is used with c a transport or contract (question-finding, Part XV (E), Derivation 5). Repair: "Write d≡_ℓ c when there are transports from d to c and from c to d, faithful at grain ℓ, each on the contract of its target" (or symmetric) — and say what a transport-content's and a contract-content's contract is.
